========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, hi. Um… what brings me in is, I guess, I’ve been getting pulled off track really easily lately. Not just “lately,” I mean for probably the past year or so. It’s like the smallest thing can knock me away from whatever I’m doing before I even have a chance to decide whether it matters.

Like if my phone buzzes, even once, I’m immediately looking at it or at least thinking about it. Or if someone just walks past my office, even if they’re not coming in or talking to me, my attention just goes with them. And sometimes it’s not even something outside of me—it can be some random thought, like something about my in-laws or some unrelated thing, and suddenly I’m off the thing I was supposed to be focused on.

It’s frustrating because I can tell myself, “Just stay with this,” but by the time I notice, I’m already somewhere else mentally. That’s really the main reason I figured I should come talk to someone.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… looking back, I think so. At the time I don’t think anyone framed it as “distracted,” exactly, or at least I didn’t understand it that way, but I was definitely the kid teachers were constantly having to manage.

Like, I remember getting moved around the classroom a lot—not because of grades necessarily, but because I’d be talking to whoever was near me, or I’d get up when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. It was almost like if something popped into my head, it was already coming out of my mouth before I had a second to stop it.

And they’d move me closer to the teacher’s desk, or away from certain kids, or put me somewhere they thought I’d be less disruptive. So, yeah, when I think back, this was definitely around when I was a kid too.

Yeah, it has. I mean, I’m still getting the main things done, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart or I’m completely unable to function at work. But it’s definitely starting to show in ways that bother me.

Those conversations should feel like I’m fully there with them, and sometimes I know I’m not giving that impression. And at home it’s kind of the same thing, just in a different way. Family plans or conversations don’t always go how they should because I’m not tracking everything, or I get pulled away mentally, and that’s started to affect trust a bit. Not in some dramatic way, but enough that I can tell people are frustrated with me. So, yeah, I’m keeping up overall, but it’s costing me more than I want to admit.

or family plans don’t go the way they should.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably harder to manage for about a year and two months, since the in-law conflict started taking over more of our family life.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / chief_executive / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi. Um… what brings me in is, I guess, I’ve been getting pulled off track really easily lately. Not just “lately,” I mean for probably the past year or so. It’s like the smallest thing can knock me away from whatever I’m doing before I even have a chance to decide whether it matters.\n\nLike if my phone buzzes, even once, I’m immediately looking at it or at least thinking about it. Or if someone just walks past my office, even if they’re not coming in or talking to me, my attention just goes with them. And sometimes it’s not even something outside of me—it can be some random thought, like something about my in-laws or some unrelated thing, and suddenly I’m off the thing I was supposed to be focused on.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I can tell myself, “Just stay with this,” but by the time I notice, I’m already somewhere else mentally. That’s really the main reason I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to grade school, were you already someone who got distracted easily?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think so. At the time I don’t think anyone framed it as “distracted,” exactly, or at least I didn’t understand it that way, but I was definitely the kid teachers were constantly having to manage.\n\nLike, I remember getting moved around the classroom a lot—not because of grades necessarily, but because I’d be talking to whoever was near me, or I’d get up when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. It was almost like if something popped into my head, it was already coming out of my mouth before I had a second to stop it.\n\nAnd they’d move me closer to the teacher’s desk, or away from certain kids, or put me somewhere they thought I’d be less disruptive. So, yeah, when I think back, this was definitely around when I was a kid too.\n\nDoctor: Has this problem with getting pulled off track been causing trouble for you at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, I’m still getting the main things done, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart or I’m completely unable to function at work. But it’s definitely starting to show in ways that bother me.\n\nMeetings are a big one. I’ll be in a meeting and I’ll miss a piece of what someone said, or I’ll get pulled into some side thing in my head, and then I have to circle back or ask them to repeat themselves. And when you’re in my kind of role, people notice that. It can come across like I don’t care, or like I’m not taking them seriously, even when I really am.\n\nIt also comes up when I’m mentoring someone or having a more one-on-one conversation. Those conversations should feel like I’m fully there with them, and sometimes I know I’m not giving that impression. People at work have been patient, but I can feel that patience wearing thin sometimes, like they’re trying not to be annoyed.\n\nAnd at home it’s kind of the same thing, just in a different way. Family plans or conversations don’t always go how they should because I’m not tracking everything, or I get pulled away mentally, and that’s started to affect trust a bit. Not in some dramatic way, but enough that I can tell people are frustrated with me. So, yeah, I’m keeping up overall, but it’s costing me more than I want to admit.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. kid too—teachers were always moving my seat because I talked too much, got up without asking, or blurted things out before they finished the question.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still keeping up overall, but it’s starting to cost me trust at home and patience from people at work when meetings, mentoring conversations, or family plans don’t go the way they should.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably harder to manage for about a year and two months, since the in-law conflict started taking over more of our family life.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / chief_executive / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you think back to grade school, were you already someone who got distracted easily?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think so. At the time I don’t think anyone framed it as “distracted,” exactly, or at least I didn’t understand it that way, but I was definitely the kid teachers were constantly having to manage.\n\nLike, I remember getting moved around the classroom a lot—not because of grades necessarily, but because I’d be talking to whoever was near me, or I’d get up when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. It was almost like if something popped into my head, it was already coming out of my mouth before I had a second to stop it.\n\nAnd they’d move me closer to the teacher’s desk, or away from certain kids, or put me somewhere they thought I’d be less disruptive. So, yeah, when I think back, this was definitely around when I was a kid too.\n\nDoctor: Has this problem with getting pulled off track been causing trouble for you at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, I’m still getting the main things done, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart or I’m completely unable to function at work. But it’s definitely starting to show in ways that bother me.\n\nMeetings are a big one. I’ll be in a meeting and I’ll miss a piece of what someone said, or I’ll get pulled into some side thing in my head, and then I have to circle back or ask them to repeat themselves. And when you’re in my kind of role, people notice that. It can come across like I don’t care, or like I’m not taking them seriously, even when I really am.\n\nIt also comes up when I’m mentoring someone or having a more one-on-one conversation. Those conversations should feel like I’m fully there with them, and sometimes I know I’m not giving that impression. People at work have been patient, but I can feel that patience wearing thin sometimes, like they’re trying not to be annoyed.\n\nAnd at home it’s kind of the same thing, just in a different way. Family plans or conversations don’t always go how they should because I’m not tracking everything, or I get pulled away mentally, and that’s started to affect trust a bit. Not in some dramatic way, but enough that I can tell people are frustrated with me. So, yeah, I’m keeping up overall, but it’s costing me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. Not “most of the day nearly every day,” at least. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, and there are definitely moments where I feel worn out or irritated because I’m not handling things the way I want to, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been depressed like that.\n\nIt’s more like I’m annoyed or discouraged when I notice the same patterns happening again, if that makes sense. But I’m not waking up every day feeling down all day or anything like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you spend a lot of time worrying about different things?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. But I’m not waking up every day feeling down all day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it “a lot,” honestly. I mean, I worry like anybody does—work stuff, family stuff, whether I handled a conversation the right way—but it doesn’t feel like I’m sitting around all day consumed by worry about everything.\n\nIt’s more that things pop into my head and I react to them, or I get frustrated in the moment. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about a bunch of different things, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did these attention problems start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it got noticeably harder after things started getting bad with my in-laws, or the conflict around them, I guess. It’s not like I woke up one day and suddenly couldn’t focus, but that’s when it really started feeling harder to manage.\n\nI’d say it’s been about a year and two months now. Around then, that whole situation started taking up a lot more space in our family life, and since then I’ve felt like staying on track takes more effort than it used to. So, yes, if we’re talking about when it became more of a problem, I’d connect it to that period.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you lose focus, is it usually because your mind goes to the situation with your in-laws?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / chief_executive / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"What made me come in is that for the past year or so, the smallest thing—a phone buzz, somebody walking past my office, or even a random thought about my in-laws—pulls me off what I’m doing before I can stop it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not usually just that, no. I mean, that situation definitely pops into my head sometimes, and when it does, it can pull me away from what I’m doing pretty quickly. Like I’ll be trying to answer an email or focus on something in my office, and then some random thought about the in-law situation comes up and suddenly I’m not really on the task anymore.

But it’s not only that. Honestly, what made me come in is more that almost anything small can knock me off track lately. My phone buzzes, somebody walks past my office, I hear something outside the door, or even just a completely random thought comes in, and before I can really stop it, I’m already distracted. Then I have to kind of drag myself back to whatever I was doing.

So the in-law stuff is part of it, but it’s not the only thing. It feels like my attention just gets grabbed too easily.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, they do sometimes. It’s not like I’m having a full flashback or anything dramatic, but a thought or memory about the conflict with my in-laws will just pop in while I’m trying to work, and then I’m off track before I’ve even really decided to think about it.

But it’s also not only that. What really pushed me to come in is that for the past year or so, the smallest things pull me away from what I’m doing—a phone buzz, someone walking past my office, a noise nearby, or even just some random thought. The in-law stuff is one of the things that grabs my attention, but it feels more like my focus is just really easy to knock loose lately.

My phone buzzes, somebody walks past my office, I hear something outside the door, or even just a completely random thought comes in, and before I can really stop it, I’m already distracted. Then I have to kind of drag myself back to whatever I was doing.\n\nSo the in-law stuff is part of it, but it’s not the only thing. It feels like my attention just gets grabbed too easily.\n\nDoctor: Do intrusive memories of the conflict with your in-laws suddenly pop into your mind when you’re trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do sometimes. It’s not like I’m having a full flashback or anything dramatic, but a thought or memory about the conflict with my in-laws will just pop in while I’m trying to work, and then I’m off track before I’ve even really decided to think about it.\n\nBut it’s also not only that. What really pushed me to come in is that for the past year or so, the smallest things pull me away from what I’m doing—a phone buzz, someone walking past my office, a noise nearby, or even just some random thought. The in-law stuff is one of the things that grabs my attention, but it feels more like my focus is just really easy to knock loose lately.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen thoughts about the conflict with your in-laws pop into your mind, do they feel distressing and unwanted?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / chief_executive / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… unwanted, yes, in the sense that I’m not sitting there trying to bring it up. It just kind of shows up in my head when I’m trying to do something else, and I’d rather it didn’t.

Distressing… maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d use that word every time. It’s more like irritating or it puts me in a bad mood, or I start replaying things and then I’m annoyed that I’m thinking about it again. It’s not like I’m panicking or anything. It just feels like, “Here we go again,” and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.

So I guess they’re unwanted, yeah. Distressing to a degree, depending on the thought, but I wouldn’t say every time it happens it feels like some big emotional hit.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. Even when I’m not upset about anything or thinking about the in-law stuff, I can still have a hard time staying with something all the way through.

Like, I’ll sit down to start a planning document, and at the beginning I’m fine — I know what I’m trying to write, I’ve got the general idea in my head, I’m moving along. But then after a few minutes, it’s like I come back to myself and realize I’ve been staring at the same section or drifting off mentally, and I don’t really know where I was going with it anymore.

Same thing in meetings with one of my managers. I can be locked in at first, listening and following what they’re saying, and then pretty soon I realize I missed half of it. Not because I didn’t care, because I do — it’s my company, these are important conversations — but I’ll suddenly notice I don’t actually know what they just said. Then I have to ask them to run it back or repeat the main part, which is embarrassing, especially in my position.

So yes, it happens even when there’s nothing particularly stressful on my mind. It’s like I can start focused, but keeping that focus steady is the hard part.

That’s really the main reason I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to grade school, were you already someone who got distracted easily?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think so. At the time I don’t think anyone framed it as “distracted,” exactly, or at least I didn’t understand it that way, but I was definitely the kid teachers were constantly having to manage.\n\nLike, I remember getting moved around the classroom a lot—not because of grades necessarily, but because I’d be talking to whoever was near me, or I’d get up when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. It was almost like if something popped into my head, it was already coming out of my mouth before I had a second to stop it.\n\nAnd they’d move me closer to the teacher’s desk, or away from certain kids, or put me somewhere they thought I’d be less disruptive. But it’s definitely starting to show in ways that bother me.\n\nMeetings are a big one. And when you’re in my kind of role, people notice that. It can come across like I don’t care, or like I’m not taking them seriously, even when I really am.\n\nIt also comes up when I’m mentoring someone or having a more one-on-one conversation. Those conversations should feel like I’m fully there with them, and sometimes I know I’m not giving that impression. Family plans or conversations don’t always go how they should because I’m not tracking everything, or I get pulled away mentally, and that’s started to affect trust a bit. But I’m not waking up every day feeling down all day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it “a lot,” honestly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I do. Probably more than I realize in the moment.

It’s mostly with interrupting, I’d say. Like I’ll jump in before someone’s finished, because I feel like I already know where they’re going or I’m afraid I’ll lose the thought if I don’t say it right then. With my wife, especially when she’s talking to her parents, I’ll cut in or answer something that wasn’t even really directed at me, and then afterward I can tell she’s irritated, like, “Could you let me handle my own conversation?”

At work it happens too. An employee will be explaining something, and I’ll finish their thought or start responding before they’ve actually landed the point. In my head I’m trying to be efficient, like, “Okay, I got it, let’s move,” but I can see on their face sometimes that it comes off rude or dismissive. And then I feel bad, because that’s not what I’m trying to do.

So yeah, I do still speak before I’ve really thought it through. Not constantly, but enough that people notice, and enough that I’m aware it rubs them the wrong way.

It’s like the smallest thing can knock me away from whatever I’m doing before I even have a chance to decide whether it matters.\n\nLike if my phone buzzes, even once, I’m immediately looking at it or at least thinking about it. Or if someone just walks past my office, even if they’re not coming in or talking to me, my attention just goes with them. And sometimes it’s not even something outside of me—it can be some random thought, like something about my in-laws or some unrelated thing, and suddenly I’m off the thing I was supposed to be focused on.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I can tell myself, “Just stay with this,” but by the time I notice, I’m already somewhere else mentally. That’s really the main reason I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to grade school, were you already someone who got distracted easily?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think so. At the time I don’t think anyone framed it as “distracted,” exactly, or at least I didn’t understand it that way, but I was definitely the kid teachers were constantly having to manage.\n\nLike, I remember getting moved around the classroom a lot—not because of grades necessarily, but because I’d be talking to whoever was near me, or I’d get up when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. It was almost like if something popped into my head, it was already coming out of my mouth before I had a second to stop it.\n\nAnd they’d move me closer to the teacher’s desk, or away from certain kids, or put me somewhere they thought I’d be less disruptive. So, yeah, when I think back, this was definitely around when I was a kid too.\n\nDoctor: Has this problem with getting pulled off track been causing trouble for you at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, I’m still getting the main things done, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart or I’m completely unable to function at work. But it’s definitely starting to show in ways that bother me.\n\nMeetings are a big one. I’ll be in a meeting and I’ll miss a piece of what someone said, or I’ll get pulled into some side thing in my head, and then I have to circle back or ask them to repeat themselves. And when you’re in my kind of role, people notice that. It can come across like I don’t care, or like I’m not taking them seriously, even when I really am.\n\nIt also comes up when I’m mentoring someone or having a more one-on-one conversation. Those conversations should feel like I’m fully there with them, and sometimes I know I’m not giving that impression. People at work have been patient, but I can feel that patience wearing thin sometimes, like they’re trying not to be annoyed.\n\nAnd at home it’s kind of the same thing, just in a different way. Family plans or conversations don’t always go how they should because I’m not tracking everything, or I get pulled away mentally, and that’s started to affect trust a bit. Not in some dramatic way, but enough that I can tell people are frustrated with me. So, yeah, I’m keeping up overall, but it’s costing me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. Not “most of the day nearly every day,” at least. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, and there are definitely moments where I feel worn out or irritated because I’m not handling things the way I want to, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been depressed like that.\n\nIt’s more like I’m annoyed or discouraged when I notice the same patterns happening again, if that makes sense. But I’m not waking up every day feeling down all day or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it “a lot,” honestly. I mean, I worry like anybody does—work stuff, family stuff, whether I handled a conversation the right way—but it doesn’t feel like I’m sitting around all day consumed by worry about everything.\n\nIt’s more that things pop into my head and I react to them, or I get frustrated in the moment. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about a bunch of different things, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did these attention problems start after a particular stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it got noticeably harder after things started getting bad with my in-laws, or the conflict around them, I guess. It’s not like I woke up one day and suddenly couldn’t focus, but that’s when it really started feeling harder to manage.\n\nI’d say it’s been about a year and two months now. Around then, that whole situation started taking up a lot more space in our family life, and since then I’ve felt like staying on track takes more effort than it used to. So, yes, if we’re talking about when it became more of a problem, I’d connect it to that period.\n\nDoctor: When you lose focus, is it usually because your mind goes to the situation with your in-laws?\n\nPatient: Not usually just that, no. I mean, that situation definitely pops into my head sometimes, and when it does, it can pull me away from what I’m doing pretty quickly. Like I’ll be trying to answer an email or focus on something in my office, and then some random thought about the in-law situation comes up and suddenly I’m not really on the task anymore.\n\nBut it’s not only that. Honestly, what made me come in is more that almost anything small can knock me off track lately. My phone buzzes, somebody walks past my office, I hear something outside the door, or even just a completely random thought comes in, and before I can really stop it, I’m already distracted. Then I have to kind of drag myself back to whatever I was doing.\n\nSo the in-law stuff is part of it, but it’s not the only thing. It feels like my attention just gets grabbed too easily.\n\nDoctor: Do intrusive memories of the conflict with your in-laws suddenly pop into your mind when you’re trying to focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do sometimes. It’s not like I’m having a full flashback or anything dramatic, but a thought or memory about the conflict with my in-laws will just pop in while I’m trying to work, and then I’m off track before I’ve even really decided to think about it.\n\nBut it’s also not only that. What really pushed me to come in is that for the past year or so, the smallest things pull me away from what I’m doing—a phone buzz, someone walking past my office, a noise nearby, or even just some random thought. The in-law stuff is one of the things that grabs my attention, but it feels more like my focus is just really easy to knock loose lately.\n\nDoctor: When thoughts about the conflict with your in-laws pop into your mind, do they feel distressing and unwanted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… unwanted, yes, in the sense that I’m not sitting there trying to bring it up. It just kind of shows up in my head when I’m trying to do something else, and I’d rather it didn’t.\n\nDistressing… maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d use that word every time. It’s more like irritating or it puts me in a bad mood, or I start replaying things and then I’m annoyed that I’m thinking about it again. It’s not like I’m panicking or anything. It just feels like, “Here we go again,” and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nSo I guess they’re unwanted, yeah. Distressing to a degree, depending on the thought, but I wouldn’t say every time it happens it feels like some big emotional hit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on a task all the way through, even when there isn’t anything stressful on your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m not upset about anything or thinking about the in-law stuff, I can still have a hard time staying with something all the way through.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to start a planning document, and at the beginning I’m fine — I know what I’m trying to write, I’ve got the general idea in my head, I’m moving along. But then after a few minutes, it’s like I come back to myself and realize I’ve been staring at the same section or drifting off mentally, and I don’t really know where I was going with it anymore.\n\nSame thing in meetings with one of my managers. I can be locked in at first, listening and following what they’re saying, and then pretty soon I realize I missed half of it. Not because I didn’t care, because I do — it’s my company, these are important conversations — but I’ll suddenly notice I don’t actually know what they just said. Then I have to ask them to run it back or repeat the main part, which is embarrassing, especially in my position.\n\nSo yes, it happens even when there’s nothing particularly stressful on my mind. It’s like I can start focused, but keeping that focus steady is the hard part.\n\nDoctor: Do you still often act or speak before you’ve had a chance to think it through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. Probably more than I realize in the moment.\n\nIt’s mostly with interrupting, I’d say. Like I’ll jump in before someone’s finished, because I feel like I already know where they’re going or I’m afraid I’ll lose the thought if I don’t say it right then. With my wife, especially when she’s talking to her parents, I’ll cut in or answer something that wasn’t even really directed at me, and then afterward I can tell she’s irritated, like, “Could you let me handle my own conversation?”\n\nAt work it happens too. An employee will be explaining something, and I’ll finish their thought or start responding before they’ve actually landed the point. In my head I’m trying to be efficient, like, “Okay, I got it, let’s move,” but I can see on their face sometimes that it comes off rude or dismissive. And then I feel bad, because that’s not what I’m trying to do.\n\nSo yeah, I do still speak before I’ve really thought it through. Not constantly, but enough that people notice, and enough that I’m aware it rubs them the wrong way."

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah, hi. Um… what brings me in is, I guess, I’ve been getting pulled off track really easily lately. Not just “lately,” I mean for probably the past year or so. It’s like the smallest thing can knock me away from whatever I’m doing before I even have a chance to decide whether it matters.\n\nLike if my phone buzzes, even once, I’m immediately looking at it or at least thinking about it. Or if someone just walks past my office, even if they’re not coming in or talking to me, my attention just goes with them. And sometimes it’s not even something outside of me—it can be some random thought, like something about my in-laws or some unrelated thing, and suddenly I’m off the thing I was supposed to be focused on.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I can tell myself, “Just stay with this,” but by the time I notice, I’m already somewhere else mentally. That’s really the main reason I figured I should come talk to someone.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.     "S060": "The patient explicitly describes moments of feeling irritated and annoyed.",
    "S024": "The patient explicitly denies being depressed most of the day nearly every day or waking up feeling down all day."
  }
}

Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t know that I’d call it “a lot,” honestly. I mean, I worry like anybody does—work stuff, family stuff, whether I handled a conversation the right way—but it doesn’t feel like I’m sitting around all day consumed by worry about everything.\n\nIt’s more that things pop into my head and I react to them, or I get frustrated in the moment. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah, they do sometimes. It’s not like I’m having a full flashback or anything dramatic, but a thought or memory about the conflict with my in-laws will just pop in while I’m trying to work, and then I’m off track before I’ve even really decided to think about it.\n\nBut it’s also not only that. What really pushed me to come in is that for the past year or so, the smallest things pull me away from what I’m doing—a phone buzz, someone walking past my office, a noise nearby, or even just some random thought. The in-law stuff is one of the things that grabs my attention, but it feels more like my focus is just really easy to knock loose lately.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.
